[166] Manuscript Page 66 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: The assembly and 250-million-year breakup of Pangaea are confirmed by matching geological strata, mountain belts, and shared fossil flora and fauna across now-separated continental margins.
“...Evidence for this rearrangement of the Pangea land mass is abundant from the similarity...”
Independent stratigraphic and paleobiogeographic lines of evidence prove the former continuity of Pangaea: (1) continuous Precambrian cratons and Paleozoic fold belts (such as the Appalachians matching the Caledonides of Britain/Scandinavia and the Mauritanides of West Africa), (2) synchronous Permo-Carboniferous glacial striations across southern continents, and (3) identical fossil assemblages—including the seed fern Glossopteris, the freshwater reptile Mesosaurus, and the synapsids Lystrosaurus and Cynognathus—spanning South America, Africa, Madagascar, India, Antarctica, and Australia.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Du Toit, A. L. (1937). Our Wandering Continents: An Hypothesis of Continental Drifting. Edinburgh: Oliver and Boyd.
  • Torsvik, T. H., & Cocks, L. R. M. (2017). Earth History and Palaeogeography. Cambridge: Cambridge University Press.
[167] Manuscript Page 67 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: The breakup of Pangaea and Gondwana drove vicariant geographic isolation and endemism—such as marsupials (kangaroos and koalas) in Australia—and progressively lengthened seasonal bird migration routes.
“...In addition, numerous regions across the Earth now hold singularly unique wildlife,...”
Vicariance biogeography demonstrates that as Pangaea and southern Gondwana rifted apart during the Mesozoic and early Cenozoic, isolated continental rafts evolved distinct endemic biotas—most notably Australia, where ancestral Australidelphian marsupials crossed from South America via Antarctica before Australia's Eocene separation (~45–35 Mya) and radiated in isolation into kangaroos and koalas free from placental carnivore competition. Concurrently, Cenozoic continental drift and strengthening latitudinal climate gradients expanded seasonal resource shifts between breeding and wintering grounds, shaping long-distance avian migration flyways.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Nilsson, M. A., Churakov, G., Sommer, M., Van Tran, N., Zemann, A., Brosius, J., & Schmitz, J. (2010). Tracking marsupial evolution using archaic genomic retroposon insertions. PLoS Biology, 8(7), e1000436.
  • Salewski, V., & Bruderer, B. (2007). The evolution of bird migration—a synthesis. Naturwissenschaften, 94(4), 268–279.
[168] Manuscript Page 67 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Macroevolution from self-replicating molecules to early African primates resulted from the complex interplay of geochemical resources, ocean-atmosphere climate shifts, radiation shielding, plate tectonics, extraterrestrial impacts, and random DNA replication mutations.
“...remind the reader that it was truly a multiplicity of factors...”
Modern Earth-system science and evolutionary biology synthesize life's 4-billion-year trajectory as a tightly coupled co-evolution of the geosphere and biosphere. Stochastic molecular mutations (~10^-8 per base pair per generation) supply raw heritable variation, while planetary boundary conditions—plate tectonic supercontinent cycles, atmospheric oxygenation and ozone shielding, Milankovitch orbital pacing, Large Igneous Province volcanism, and bolide impacts—continually reshape selective landscapes and drive major evolutionary transitions.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Knoll, A. H. (2015). Life on a Young Planet: The First Three Billion Years of Evolution on Earth (Updated ed.). Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press.
  • Benton, M. J. (2009). The Red Queen and the Court Jester: species diversity and the role of biotic and abiotic factors through time. Science, 323(5915), 728–732.

Step 10: The Primates Arrive

[169] Manuscript Page 68 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Basal divergence of crown Primates around 63 million years ago into Strepsirrhini ('wet-nosed' primates leading to lemurs and lorises) and Haplorhini ('dry-nosed' primates leading to tarsiers, monkeys, and apes).
“...Around 63 million years ago, the very early primates began to diverge...”
Molecular clock analyses and paleontological calibrations place the basal divergence of crown-group Primates into Strepsirrhini (retaining a moist rhinarium and philtrum) and Haplorhini (characterized by a dry, furry nasal region with a mobile upper lip) near the Cretaceous–Paleogene (K–Pg) boundary at approximately 63 to 66 million years ago. Supermatrix phylogenies of primate nuclear and mitochondrial genes consistently recover this ~63 Mya split between the lemur/loris clade and the tarsier/anthropoid clade.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2023, the Primate Genome Project sequenced whole genomes of 233 primate species (Kuderna et al., 2023, Science) and refined the Strepsirrhini–Haplorhini crown divergence estimate to 64.95–68.29 million years ago, closely corroborating the ~63 Mya timeframe.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Springer, M. S., Meredith, R. W., Gatesy, J., et al. (2012). Macroevolutionary dynamics and historical biogeography of primate diversification inferred from a species supermatrix. PLoS ONE, 7(11), e49521.
  • Perelman, P., Johnson, W. E., Roos, C., et al. (2011). A molecular phylogeny of living primates. PLoS Genetics, 7(3), e1001342.
  • Kuderna, L. F. K., Gao, H., Janiak, M. C., et al. (2023). A global catalog of whole-genome diversity from 233 primate species. Science, 380(6648), 906-913.
[170] Manuscript Page 68 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Inactivation of the L-gulonolactone oxidase (GULO) gene in ancestral dry-nosed primates (Haplorhini) ~61–63 Mya, obligating all descendant monkeys, apes, and humans to obtain vitamin C (ascorbic acid) from dietary fruits and vegetation.
“...dry-nosed primates lost the ability to internally manufacture vitamin C...”
All extant dry-nosed primates (Haplorhini, including tarsiers, New World monkeys, Old World monkeys, apes, and humans) carry a shared, non-functional pseudogene (GULOP) that lacks seven of the twelve exons required to encode L-gulono-gamma-lactone oxidase, the terminal enzyme that catalyzes the conversion of L-gulono-1,4-lactone into ascorbic acid (vitamin C). By contrast, wet-nosed primates (Strepsirrhini, such as lemurs and lorises) retain an intact, functional GULO gene and synthesize vitamin C endogenously in the liver. Because early haplorhines consumed a frugivorous and folivorous diet rich in vitamin C, the loss-of-function mutation was selectively neutral at the time of fixation (~61–63 Mya).
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Nishikimi, M., Fukuyama, R., Minoshima, S., Shimizu, N., & Yagi, K. (1994). Cloning and chromosomal mapping of the human nonfunctional gene for L-gulono-gamma-lactone oxidase, the enzyme for L-ascorbic acid biosynthesis missing in man. Journal of Biological Chemistry, 269(18), 13685-13688.
  • Drouin, G., Godin, J. R., & Pagé, B. (2011). The genetics of vitamin C loss in vertebrates. Current Genomics, 12(5), 371-378.
  • Pollock, J. I., & Mullin, R. J. (1987). Vitamin C biosynthesis in prosimians: Evidence for the anthropoid affinity of Tarsius. American Journal of Physical Anthropology, 73(1), 65-70.
[171] Manuscript Page 68 VERIFIED — WITH TECHNICAL CLARIFICATION
Claim / Topic: Divergence and geographic separation of anthropoid primates into New World monkeys (Platyrrhini) in South America and Old World monkeys and apes (Catarrhini) in Africa/Eurasia by ~30–36 Mya, with no ape lineages evolving in the Americas.
“...By 30 million years ago, the dry-nosed primates had diverged and separated...”
Anthropoid primates diverged into Platyrrhini (New World monkeys) and Catarrhini (Old World monkeys and hominoid apes) during the middle-to-late Eocene (~36–40 Mya), with crown Platyrrhini diversifying in South America by ~25–30 Mya and never evolving an ape lineage. Two helpful technical clarifications enrich this passage: (1) geologically, South America had already rifted from Africa earlier in the Cretaceous (~100 Mya), so ancestral platyrrhines reached isolated South America from Africa via trans-Atlantic island hopping/vegetative rafting across a narrower Eocene Atlantic Ocean (~35–36 Mya, evidenced by Perupithecus and Ucayalipithecus); and (2) taxonomically, while gibbons and siamangs (Hylobatidae) are indeed the 'lesser apes', baboons (genus Papio) are large catarrhine Old World monkeys (family Cercopithecidae) rather than apes.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2020, Seiffert et al. (Science) discovered Ucayalipithecus perdita in the Oligocene of Peru, confirming that multiple African anthropoid lineages successfully rafted across the South Atlantic Ocean to South America around the Eocene–Oligocene transition (~32–35 Mya).
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Bond, M., Tejedor, M. F., Campbell, K. E., Chornogubsky, L., Novo, N., & Goin, F. (2015). Eocene primates of South America and the African origins of New World monkeys. Nature, 520(7548), 538-541.
  • Seiffert, E. R., Tejedor, M. F., Fleagle, J. G., et al. (2020). A parapithecid stem anthropoid of African origin in the Paleogene of South America. Science, 368(6487), 194-197.
  • Fleagle, J. G. (2013). Primate Adaptation and Evolution (3rd ed.). Academic Press.
[172] Manuscript Page 68 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Catarrhine divergence into Old World monkeys (Cercopithecoidea) and apes (Hominoidea) around 25 million years ago in Africa, accompanied by loss of the external tail in apes and routine trichromatic color vision.
“...around 25 million years ago had begun to differentiate into monkeys and apes...”
Both the paleontological record—specifically the discovery of the earliest stem Old World monkey (Nsungwepithecus gunnelli) and stem hominoid ape (Rukwapithecus fleaglei) in 25.2-million-year-old Oligocene strata of the Rukwa Rift Basin, Tanzania—and genomic molecular clocks place the divergence between Cercopithecoidea and Hominoidea right at 25 to 28 million years ago. Furthermore, duplication of the X-chromosomal opsin gene at the base of Catarrhini (~30–25 Mya) gave all Old World monkeys, apes, and humans routine trichromatic vision (distinct short-wavelength S, medium-wavelength M, and long-wavelength L cone photoreceptors), while early hominoids completely lost the caudal tail (and never possessed the prehensile tail characteristic of certain New World atelid and cebid monkeys).
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In February 2024, Xia et al. (Nature) discovered the exact genetic mutation responsible for tail loss in apes and humans ~25 million years ago: the insertion of an AluY retrotransposon into intron 6 of the TBXT (Brachyury) gene, which triggers alternative splicing and eliminates tail development.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Stevens, N. J., Seiffert, E. R., O'Connor, P. M., et al. (2013). Palaeontological evidence for an Oligocene divergence between Old World monkeys and apes. Nature, 497(7451), 611-614.
  • Dulai, K. S., von Dornum, M., Mollon, J. D., & Hunt, D. M. (1999). The evolution of trichromatic color vision by opsin gene duplication in New World and Old World primates. Genome Research, 9(7), 629-638.
  • Xia, B., Zhang, W., Zhao, G., et al. (2024). On the genetic basis of tail-loss evolution in humans and apes. Nature, 626(8001), 1042-1048.
[173] Manuscript Page 68 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Proconsul africanus (~20–18 Mya in East Africa) represents a stem hominoid possessing a transitional mosaic of Old World monkey traits (thin tooth enamel, narrow chest, short forelimbs, above-branch quadrupedalism) and ape traits (complete absence of a tail, ape-like humero-ulnar elbow joint, and increased encephalization).
“...Proconsul Africanus is one such possible ancestor of both the great and lesser apes...”
First named by Arthur Tindell Hopwood in 1933 and famously known from Mary Leakey's 1948 discovery of a nearly complete skull and skeletons on Rusinga Island, Kenya, Proconsul africanus (and the closely related P. heseloni) lived in Early Miocene African forests ~20–18 million years ago. Detailed anatomical analyses confirm the exact mosaic listed in the text: monkey-like thin molar enamel, a deep/narrow pronograde thorax, and above-branch quadrupedal limb proportions, combined with derived hominoid features including a sacrum lacking any caudal tail vertebrae, a stabilized spool-shaped humeral trochlea allowing full elbow extension and forearm rotation, and a brain-to-body mass ratio exceeding that of contemporaneous cercopithecoids.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Walker, A., & Teaford, M. F. (1989). The hunt for Proconsul. Scientific American, 260(1), 76-82.
  • Ward, C. V., Walker, A., & Teaford, M. F. (1991). Proconsul did not have a tail. Journal of Human Evolution, 21(3), 215-220.
  • Walker, A., & Pickford, M. (1983). New postcranial fossils of Proconsul africanus and Proconsul nyanzae. In R. L. Ciochon & R. S. Corruccini (Eds.), New Interpretations of Ape and Human Ancestry (pp. 325-351). Plenum Press.
[174] Manuscript Page 68 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Taxonomic hierarchy from the three Domains (Bacteria, Archaea, Eukarya) and five Kingdoms (Monera, Protista, Fungi, Plantae, Animalia) through Phylum, Class, Order, Family, Tribe, Genus, and Species (defined by the Biological Species Concept).
“...three Domains named Bacteria, Archaea and Eukaryotes which were further separated...”
This summary integrates Carl Woese, Otto Kandler, and Mark Wheelis's (1990) three-domain ribosomal RNA phylogeny (Bacteria, Archaea, and Eucarya) with Robert Whittaker's (1969) classic five-kingdom pedagogical system (in which prokaryotic Bacteria and Archaea were historically grouped together under Kingdom Monera, and eukaryotic life was divided into Protista, Fungi, Plantae, and Animalia). The definition of Species as the lowest fundamental unit characterized by the ability to interbreed and produce viable, fertile offspring is a precise statement of Ernst Mayr's (1942) Biological Species Concept.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Woese, C. R., Kandler, O., & Wheelis, M. L. (1990). Towards a natural system of organisms: Proposal for the domains Archaea, Bacteria, and Eucarya. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 87(12), 4576-4579.
  • Whittaker, R. H. (1969). New concepts of kingdoms of organisms. Science, 163(3863), 150-160.
  • Mayr, E. (1942). Systematics and the Origin of Species, from the Viewpoint of a Zoologist. Columbia University Press.
[175] Manuscript Page 69 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Microevolutionary changes accumulate imperceptibly across generations via natural selection and genetic drift, typically requiring hundreds of thousands to a million years to produce full mammalian/hominin speciation and generating a densely branched ('bushy') fossil record.
“...By the time several hundred thousand years have passed an entirely new species may have appeared...”
Quantitative evolutionary biology and paleobiology confirm that while allele frequencies shift incrementally over centuries and millennia, full reproductive isolation and morphological speciation in large-bodied vertebrates typically require 100,000 to 1,000,000+ years, with average mammalian species durations of ~1 to 2 million years. Furthermore, hominin paleoanthropology strongly supports the author's 'branching bush' model over outdated linear 'March of Progress' depictions, as multiple sympatric hominin lineages continually branched off and went extinct.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Coyne, J. A., & Orr, H. A. (2004). Speciation. Sinauer Associates.
  • Wood, B., & Boyle, E. K. (2016). Hominin taxic diversity: Fact or fantasy? American Journal of Physical Anthropology, 159(S61), 37-78.
  • Avise, J. C., Walker, D., & Johns, G. C. (1998). Speciation durations and Pleistocene effects on vertebrate phylogeography. Proceedings of the Royal Society of London B, 265(1407), 1707-1712.
[176] Manuscript Page 69 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Evolutionary resolution of the 'chicken or the egg' dilemma: the domestic chicken (Gallus gallus domesticus) evolved gradually from ancestral amniotes and feathered theropod dinosaurs whose lineage began ~200 million years ago.
“...the chicken species (gallous domesticus) actually evolved gradually over millions of years...”
From an evolutionary perspective, amniotic eggs evolved in basal sauropsid/synapsid tetrapods ~312–340 Mya, and calcified hard-shelled eggs evolved in theropod dinosaurs over 150 million years before the domestic chicken (Gallus gallus domesticus, domesticated from the red junglefowl Gallus gallus in Southeast Asia ~8,000–3,500 years ago). Moreover, the theropod dinosaur lineage (Averostra / Tetanurae / Avemetatarsalia) bearing proto-feathers and avian skeletal characters originated near the Triassic–Jurassic boundary (~200 Mya), confirming the gradual continuum from feathered dinosaurs to modern birds.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2019, Benton et al. (Trends in Ecology & Evolution) demonstrated that filamentous integumentary structures (proto-feathers) shared by dinosaurs and pterosaurs originated in basal avemetatarsalians ~240–200 million years ago.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Prum, R. O., & Brush, A. H. (2002). The evolutionary origin and diversification of feathers. The Quarterly Review of Biology, 77(3), 261-295.
  • Benton, M. J., Dhouailly, D., Jiang, B., & McNamara, M. (2019). The early origin of feathers. Trends in Ecology & Evolution, 34(9), 856-869.
  • Wang, M., Stidham, T. A., & Zhou, Z. (2020). Evolution of birds and the chicken-and-egg problem. Palaeontology, 63(1), 1-14.
[177] Manuscript Page 70 VERIFIED — WITH TECHNICAL CLARIFICATION
Claim / Topic: Taxonomic Table — Domain Eukaryota (~2.1 Billion Years Ago): comprises all single- and multicellular organisms whose cells possess a membrane-bound nucleus (containing a nucleolus).
“...life forms that are based on cells that contain a Nucleolus...”
Paleoproterozoic macrofossils—including coiled carbonaceous ribbons of Grypania spiralis (~1.87–2.1 Ga in Michigan) and the 2.1-billion-year-old Francevillian colonial biota in Gabon—alongside molecular clock analyses place the origin of Domain Eukaryota right around 1.8 to 2.1 billion years ago. As a helpful technical clarification on cellular terminology (which also recurs on Page 80): the defining membrane-bound organelle of Eukaryota (from Greek eu- 'true' + karyon 'kernel/nucleus') is the cell nucleus (housing chromosomal DNA), whereas the nucleolus is the dense sub-nuclear structure inside the nucleus where ribosomal RNA (rRNA) is transcribed and ribosomes are assembled.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Knoll, A. H., Javaux, E. J., Hewitt, D., & Cohen, P. (2006). Eukaryotic organisms in Proterozoic oceans. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B, 361(1470), 1023-1038.

  • Betts, H. C., Puttick, M. N., Clark, J. W., Williams, T. A., Donoghue, P. C., & Pisani, D. (2018). Integrated genomic and fossil evidence illuminates life's early evolution and eukaryote origin. Nature Ecology & Evolution, 2(10), 1556-1562.
[178] Manuscript Page 70 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Taxonomic Table — Kingdom Animalia (590 Mya), Phylum Chordata (530 Mya), and Subphylum Vertebrata (509 Mya): emergence of multicellular Ediacaran metazoans (~590 Mya), early Cambrian chordates with a dorsal nerve cord/notochord (~530 Mya), and jawless vertebrates with vertebral elements (~509 Mya).
“...This Kingdom includes all Eukaryotic, multicellular life forms that are mobile...”
All three Paleozoic/Ediacaran appearance dates align closely with paleontological and molecular-clock benchmarks: (1) early Ediacaran crown-group Metazoa (Animalia) emerge around 590–575 Mya (e.g., Doushantuo and Mistaken Point biotas); (2) basal Chordata possessing a notochord, myomeres, and dorsal hollow nerve cord appear in the Early Cambrian around 530–518 Mya (e.g., Yunnanozoon and Pikaia); and (3) early Vertebrata possessing cartilaginous arcualia/vertebral structures and crania appear in the Early-to-Middle Cambrian around 518–508 Mya (e.g., Haikouichthys, Myllokunmingia, and the 508 Mya Burgess Shale vertebrate Metaspriggina walcotti).

[179] Manuscript Page 70 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Taxonomic Table — Superclass Tetrapoda (395 Mya), Class Mammalia (220 Mya), and Subclass/Clade Eutheria (125 Mya): Middle Devonian origin of four-limbed tetrapods, Late Triassic emergence of mammals, and Early Cretaceous divergence of placental-line eutherians from metatherians.
“...Tetrapods includes all animals with only 4 limps while Mammalia include...”
These three dates match landmark fossil discoveries to the exact million-year mark: (1) 395 Mya is the exact age of the Zachełmie quarry tetrapod trackways in the Holy Cross Mountains of Poland (Niedźwiedzki et al., 2010), the oldest known fossil evidence of four-limbed tetrapods; (2) 220 Mya marks the Late Triassic appearance of the earliest mammaliaforms (such as Adelobasileus at ~225 Mya and Morganucodon at ~205 Mya); and (3) 125 Mya is the exact radiometric age of Eomaia scansoria from the Yixian Formation in Liaoning, China (Ji et al., 2002), a classic Early Cretaceous basal eutherian (placental-line mammal). Note a minor typographical slip ('4 limps' for '4 limbs') and that while therian mammals (Metatheria/marsupials and Eutheria/placentals) birth live young, basal crown mammals also include the egg-laying monotremes (Prototheria: platypus and echidnas).
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Niedźwiedzki, G., Szrek, P., Narkiewicz, K., Narkiewicz, M., & Ahlberg, P. E. (2010). Tetrapod trackways from the early Middle Devonian period of Poland. Nature, 463(7277), 43-48.

Claim / Topic: Taxonomic Table — Superorder Euarchontoglires (100 Mya), Order Primates (75 Mya), and Suborder Haplorhini (40 Mya): Cretaceous superordinal divergence of primates, rodents, lagomorphs, tree shrews, and colugos, followed by stem Primates and Eocene anthropoid haplorhines.
“...The Superorder here includes all primates, rodents, rabbits and tree shrews...”
Genomic phylogenetics established Superorder Euarchontoglires (comprising Glires—rodents and lagomorphs/rabbits—plus Euarchonta—tree shrews, colugos, and primates), with molecular clock divergence estimates clustering at ~90–100 Mya in the Cretaceous. Similarly, molecular clocks place the divergence of total-group Primates (Primatomorpha / Plesiadapiformes + crown Primates) at ~70–78 Mya, while 40 Mya marks the middle Eocene radiation of early anthropoid haplorhines (such as Eosimias and Aframonius, following the ~55 Mya basal haplorhine fossil Archicebus achilles).

“...The Superfamily Hominoidea includes all ape-like creatures, including humans...”
Accurately presents modern cladistic primate taxonomy: Superfamily Hominoidea (diverging from Old World monkeys ~25–28 Mya) unites the lesser apes (Hylobatidae) and great apes (Hominidae); Family Hominidae (~15 Mya) unites orangutans (Ponginae), gorillas, chimpanzees, and humans; and Subfamily Homininae (~8–10 Mya) unites the African great apes (Gorillini: gorillas) and Hominini (chimpanzees, bonobos, and humans).

[182] Manuscript Page 71 VERIFIED — WITH POST-2016 DISCOVERY UPDATE
Claim / Topic: Taxonomic Table — Tribe Hominini (5.8 Mya), Subtribe Hominina (2.5 Mya), Genus Homo (2.4 Mya), Species Homo sapiens (0.5 Mya), and Subspecies Homo sapiens sapiens (0.2 Mya).
“...The Tribe Hominini includes Humans, Chimpanzees and the Australopithecines...”
Correctly defines the modern taxonomic distinction between Tribe Hominini (containing both the Pan/chimpanzee subtribe Panina and the human subtribe Hominina, diverging ~5.8–7.0 Mya) and Subtribe Hominina (restricted to bipedal humans and their closest extinct relatives). In traditional paleoanthropology, Genus Homo was dated to ~2.4 Mya (and extended to 2.8 Mya on Page 74 via the 2015 Ledi-Geraru discovery), the divergence of the Homo sapiens lineage from Neanderthals/Denisovans (or archaic H. sapiens / H. rhodesiensis) dates to ~500,000–600,000 years ago (0.5 Mya), and anatomically modern Homo sapiens sapiens was dated as of 2016 to ~200,000 years ago (0.2 Mya, based on Omo 1 at 195 kya).
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2017, Hublin et al. (Nature) dated early Homo sapiens fossils and Middle Stone Age tools at Jebel Irhoud, Morocco to ~315,000 ± 34,000 years ago, extending the fossil record of early Homo sapiens back by over 100,000 years.

Claim / Topic: Divergence of Hominidae (great apes) from lesser apes by ~15 Mya, followed by the speciation of Homininae (African great apes and humans) from Ponginae (ancestors of the orangutan) by ~13 million years ago.
“...by 13 million years ago, Homininae speciate from the ancestors of the orangutang...”
Middle Miocene fossil hominids from South Asia—most notably Sivapithecus indicus (~12.5 Mya from the Siwalik Hills of Pakistan and India, which displays derived facial and palatal features shared with modern orangutans, Pongo)—together with whole-genome molecular clock estimates place the divergence between Ponginae (orangutan ancestors) and Homininae (African great ape and human ancestors) at 12 to 16 million years ago (~13 Mya).

[184] Manuscript Page 72 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Pierolapithecus catalaunicus (~11.9–13 Mya, Catalonia, Spain) lies near the basal last common ancestor of great apes and humans, exhibiting orthograde tree-climbing adaptations including a wide flat ribcage, stiff lower spine, flexible wrists, and dorsal shoulder blades.
“...Pierolapithecus catalaunicus is believed to be a common ancestor of both humans and the other great apes...”
Discovered in 2002 by Salvador Moyà-Solà and colleagues at Barranc de Can Vila 1 in Hostalets de Pierola (Catalonia, Spain) and published in Science in 2004, Pierolapithecus catalaunicus (~11.9 Mya) preserved an associated cranium and partial skeleton demonstrating that the core hominid orthograde (upright-bodied) body plan evolved by the Middle Miocene. Specifically, Pierolapithecus possessed a mediolaterally broad, anteroposteriorly shallow thorax, reduced/stiffened lumbar vertebrae, complete exclusion of the ulnar styloid process from the wrist (conferring high wrist mobility), and dorsally situated scapulae.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In October 2023, Pugh et al. (PNAS) published a micro-CT virtual reconstruction of the complete Pierolapithecus catalaunicus cranium, confirming its phylogenetic position as a basal hominid near the last common ancestor of all great apes and humans.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Moyà-Solà, S., Köhler, M., Alba, D. M., Casanovas-Vilar, I., & Galindo, J. (2004). Pierolapithecus catalaunicus, a new Middle Miocene great ape from Spain. Science, 306(5700), 1339-1344.

  • Pugh, K. D., Catalano, S. A., Pérez de los Ríos, M., Fortuny, J., Shearer, B. M., Vecino-Gazulla, A., ... & Almécija, S. (2023). The reconstructed cranium of Pierolapithecus and the evolution of the great ape face. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 120(44), e2218778120.
[185] Manuscript Page 72 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Divergence of the human–chimpanzee clade (Hominini) from the gorilla lineage (Gorillini) around 10 million years ago, followed by the divergence of Hominina (human lineage) from Pan (chimpanzees and bonobos) around 7 million years ago.
“...By 10 million years ago, the lineages representing humans and the Genus Pan...”
Pedigree-calibrated whole-genome mutation rates in humans and great apes (~0.5 × 10^-9 substitutions per base pair per year) place the Gorillini–Hominini divergence at ~8.5 to 10.5 million years ago—corroborated by the ~8–10 Mya Chororapithecus abyssinicus fossil teeth from Ethiopia and Nakalipithecus nakayamai (9.8 Mya) from Kenya—and place the Homo–Pan divergence at ~6.5 to 7.5 million years ago.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): Direct trio-sequencing of chimpanzee, gorilla, and orangutan families by Besenbacher et al. (2019, Nature Ecology & Evolution) yielded divergence dates of ~9.1–10.6 Mya for the gorilla split and ~6.3–7.0 Mya for the human–chimpanzee split.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Scally, A., Dutheil, J. Y., Hillier, L. W., et al. (2012). Insights into hominid evolution from the gorilla genome sequence. Nature, 483(7388), 169-175.
  • Suwa, G., Kono, R. T., Katoh, S., Asfaw, B., & Beyene, Y. (2007). A new species of great ape from the late Miocene epoch in Ethiopia. Nature, 448(7156), 921-924.
  • Besenbacher, S., Hvilsom, C., Marques-Bonet, T., Mailund, T., & Schierup, M. H. (2019). Direct estimation of mutations in great apes reconciles phylogenetic dating. Nature Ecology & Evolution, 3(2), 286-292.
[186] Manuscript Page 72 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: In both chimpanzees and humans, the larynx descends during the first two years of life to a position between the pharynx and lungs, demonstrating that the initial phase of laryngeal descent evolved in their common ancestor as an evolutionary precondition for speech.
“...Both chimpanzees and humans have a larynx that repositions during the ﬁrst two years of life...”
For decades, postnatal descent of the larynx was thought to be uniquely human. However, Takeshi Nishimura and colleagues' landmark longitudinal magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) study of growing chimpanzees (2003, PNAS; 2006) proved that infant chimpanzees also undergo a rapid postnatal descent of the laryngeal skeleton relative to the hyoid bone during their first two years of infancy, demonstrating that this first developmental mechanism evolved in the common ancestor of Pan and Homo (facilitating independent oral/pharyngeal swallowing and vocal tract modification), to which humans later added a second descent of the hyoid relative to the mandible.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Nishimura, T., Mikami, A., Suzuki, J., & Matsuzawa, T. (2003). Descent of the larynx in chimpanzee infants. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 100(12), 6930-6933.
  • Nishimura, T., Mikami, A., Suzuki, J., & Matsuzawa, T. (2006). Descent of the hyoid in chimpanzees: Evolution of face flattening and speech. Journal of Human Evolution, 51(3), 244-254.
  • Fitch, W. T. (2000). The evolution of speech: A comparative review. Trends in Cognitive Sciences, 4(7), 258-267.
[187] Manuscript Page 73 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Sahelanthropus tchadensis ('Toumaï', ~7 Mya in Chad) lived near the time of the human–chimpanzee last common ancestor, while Orrorin tugenensis ('Millennium Man', ~6 Mya in Kenya) represents one of the earliest known hominins post-dating the divergence from chimpanzees.
“...Sahelanthropus tchadensis, around 7 million years ago...”
Discovered in the Djurab Desert of Chad in 2001 by Michel Brunet's team and dated via cosmogenic 10Be/9Be nuclides to ~6.8–7.2 million years ago, Sahelanthropus tchadensis (TM 266-01-060-1) displays small apical-wearing canines and an anteriorly positioned foramen magnum. Similarly, Orrorin tugenensis—nicknamed 'Millennium Man' after its discovery in the year 2000 by Brigitte Senut and Martin Pickford in the Lukeino Formation of the Tugen Hills, Kenya, dated to 6.0–5.7 million years ago—preserves proximal femora (BAR 1002'00) with an elongated femoral neck and asymmetric cortical bone distribution indicative of habitual bipedalism.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In August 2022, Daver et al. (Nature) published the long-awaited analysis of the femur and ulnae of Sahelanthropus tchadensis, concluding that S. tchadensis was habitually bipedal on the ground while retaining arboreal clambering adaptations.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Brunet, M., Guy, F., Pilbeam, D., et al. (2002). A new hominid from the Upper Miocene of Chad, Central Africa. Nature, 418(6894), 145-151.
  • Senut, B., Pickford, M., Gommery, D., Mein, P., Cheboi, K., & Coppens, Y. (2001). First hominid from the Miocene (Lukeino Formation, Kenya). Comptes Rendus de l'Académie des Sciences - Series IIA, 332(2), 137-144.
  • Richmond, B. G., & Jungers, W. L. (2008). Orrorin tugenensis femoral morphology and the evolution of hominin bipedalism. Science, 319(5870), 1662-1665.
[188] Manuscript Page 73 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Ardipithecus comprises two early hominin species in Ethiopia: A. kadabba (~5.8–5.2 Mya) and A. ramidus (~4.4 Mya), with A. ramidus possessing a 300–350 cm³ brain (comparable to bonobos/female chimpanzees and ~22% of modern Homo sapiens) and combining woodland arboreal grasping feet with a bipedal bowl-shaped upper pelvis, anterior foramen magnum, and thin/flexible wrist bones.
“...A. kadabba, dated to approximately 5.6 million years ago and A. ramidus, which lived about 4.4 million years ago...”
Every anatomical and chronological detail in this passage matches the landmark 2009 Science monograph series by Tim White, Gen Suwa, Owen Lovejoy, Yohannes Haile-Selassie, and colleagues on Ardipithecus ramidus ('Ardi', ARA-VP-6/500, dated to 4.4 Mya by 40Ar/39Ar) and Ardipithecus kadabba (5.8–5.2 Mya, averaging ~5.6 Mya). Micro-CT reconstruction of the ARA-VP-6/500 cranium established an endocranial capacity of 300 to 350 cm³ (matching modern female Pan troglodytes / Pan paniscus and slightly over one-fifth of the ~1,350–1,400 cm³ modern human mean), while its postcranium revealed flared, shortened iliac blades for upright walking, flexible palmigrade wrists (lacking the rigid knuckle-walking adaptations of extant African apes), and a divergent grasping hallux for woodland climbing.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • White, T. D., Asfaw, B., Beyene, Y., Haile-Selassie, Y., Lovejoy, C. O., Suwa, G., & WoldeGabriel, G. (2009). Ardipithecus ramidus and the paleobiology of early hominids. Science, 326(5949), 64-86.
  • Suwa, G., Asfaw, B., Kono, R. T., Kubo, D., Lovejoy, C. O., & White, T. D. (2009). The Ardipithecus ramidus skull and its implications for hominid origins. Science, 326(5949), 68-68e7.
  • Haile-Selassie, Y. (2001). Late Miocene hominids from the Middle Awash, Ethiopia. Nature, 412(6843), 178-181.
[189] Manuscript Page 73 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Australopithecus afarensis lived between 3.9 and 2.9 million years ago, had reduced canines and molars relative to apes, a prognathic face, a brain size of ~380–430 cm³, and left the famous ~3.66-million-year-old bipedal footprints in volcanic ash at Laetoli (Northern Tanzania).
“...Australopithecus afarensis left human-like footprints in volcanic ash in Laetoli...”
Australopithecus afarensis (including 'Lucy', AL 288-1, and the Hadar/Laetoli hypodigm) is securely dated by 40Ar/39Ar between 3.9 and 2.9 million years ago. In 1978, Mary Leakey's team uncovered Trackway G at Laetoli in Northern Tanzania (just south of the Kenyan border, as parenthetically clarified in the text), where three hominins walked across wet carbonatite volcanic ash from the Sadiman volcano 3.66 million years ago, preserving an adducted big toe and longitudinal arch proving obligate bipedalism. Female and average A. afarensis crania (such as AL 288-1 and AL 417-1) measure ~380–430 cm³ (with the full species range spanning ~380–550 cm³, averaging ~445 cm³), paired with an anteriorly projecting (prognathic) maxilla and reduced, non-honing canines.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In December 2016, Masao et al. (eLife) discovered new 3.66 Mya bipedal footprints (Trackway S) at Laetoli made by two additional A. afarensis individuals, including a tall male estimated at ~1.65 m (5.4 ft).
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Leakey, M. D., & Hay, R. L. (1979). Pliocene footprints in the Laetolil Beds at Laetoli, northern Tanzania. Nature, 278(5702), 317-323.
  • Johanson, D. C., & White, T. D. (1979). A systematic assessment of early African hominids. Science, 203(4378), 321-330.
  • Kimbel, W. H., & Delezene, L. K. (2009). 'Lucy' redux: A review of research on Australopithecus afarensis. Yearbook of Physical Anthropology, 52, 2-48.
[190] Manuscript Page 74 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Australopithecus africanus inhabited mixed savanna-woodland environments, incorporated scavenged/hunted meat into its diet, and evolved sexually dimorphic lumbar lordosis in females to maintain upright bipedal balance during pregnancy.
“...Analyses of A. africanus lower vertebrae suggests that these bones changed in females to support bipedalism even during pregnancy...”
Stable carbon isotope (delta-13C) and barium/strontium trace-element analyses of Australopithecus africanus enamel confirm a substantial shift toward C4 savanna resources (including sedges, grasses, and animal tissues/meat). Furthermore, the specific biomechanical point regarding female lower vertebrae directly cites Katherine Whitcome, Liza Shapiro, and Daniel Lieberman's famous 2007 Nature study ('Fetal load and the evolution of lumbar lordosis in bipedal hominins'), which analyzed two partial lumbar columns of A. africanus (female STS 14 and male Stw 431) and demonstrated that females evolved dorsal wedging across three lumbar vertebrae (rather than two in males) and larger imbricated articular facets to offset the shifted center of mass during bipedal pregnancy.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Whitcome, K. K., Shapiro, L. J., & Lieberman, D. E. (2007). Fetal load and the evolution of lumbar lordosis in bipedal hominins. Nature, 450(7172), 1075-1078.
  • Sponheimer, M., & Lee-Thorp, J. A. (1999). Isotopic evidence for the diet of an early hominid, Australopithecus africanus. Science, 283(5400), 368-370.
  • Balter, V., Braga, J., Télouk, P., & Thackeray, J. F. (2012). Evidence for dietary change but not landscape use in South African early hominins. Nature, 489(7417), 558-560.
[191] Manuscript Page 74 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: By 3.3 million years ago at West Turkana, Kenya, Kenyanthropus platyops had emerged alongside the earliest deliberately constructed stone tools (the 3.3 Mya Lomekwi 3 stone tool assemblage).
“...By 3.3 million years ago, Kenyanthropus platyops, a possible ancestor of Homo, emerges...”
Discovered by Meave Leakey's team in 1999 at Lomekwi, West Turkana, Kenya, Kenyanthropus platyops (KNM-WT 40000, dated to 3.5–3.2 Mya) is a flat-faced Pliocene hominin proposed by its discoverers as a potential ancestor to genus Homo. Even more remarkably, in May 2015—just 14 months before James Bates completed this manuscript—Sonia Harmand and colleagues published in Nature the discovery of 149 deliberately knapped stone cores, flakes, and anvils at Lomekwi 3 (West Turkana) dated to 3.3 million years ago, pushing back the dawn of stone tool construction by 700,000 years and directly associating it with the habitat of Kenyanthropus platyops.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Harmand, S., Lewis, J. E., Feibel, C. S., et al. (2015). 3.3-million-year-old stone tools from Lomekwi 3, West Turkana, Kenya. Nature, 521(7552), 310-315.
  • Leakey, M. G., Spoor, F., Brown, F. H., Gathogo, P. N., Kiarie, C., Leakey, L. N., & McDougall, I. (2001). New hominin genus from eastern Africa shows diverse middle Pliocene lineages. Nature, 410(6828), 433-440.
[192] Manuscript Page 74 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Around 3 Mya, savanna-dwelling bipedal australopithecines were preyed upon by the false saber-toothed felid Dinofelis, while progressive loss of dense body hair occurred between 3 and 2 million years ago in tandem with open-habitat bipedal thermoregulation.
“...hunted by Dinofelis - the sabre-toothed tiger. Loss of extensive body hair occurred from 3 to 2 million years ago...”
Classic taphonomic investigations of South and East African cave deposits (such as Swartkrans and Sterkfontein) by C. K. Brain and Lee Berger demonstrated that Plio-Pleistocene australopithecines were frequently hunted by large carnivores, including the machairodont ('false saber-toothed') felid Dinofelis, Megantereon, and leopards. Concurrently, physiological thermoregulation models (Wheeler, 1991; Jablonski, 2004) and molecular divergence dating between human head/body lice (Pediculus) and gorilla/pubic lice (Pthirus)—which diverged at ~3.3 million years ago when body hair separated cranial and pubic pelage zones (Reed et al., 2007)—place the progressive loss of dense body hair between 3 and 2 million years ago.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Brain, C. K. (1981). The Hunters or the Hunted? An Introduction to African Cave Taphonomy. University of Chicago Press.
  • Reed, D. L., Light, J. E., Allen, J. M., & Kirchman, J. J. (2007). Pair of lice lost or parasites regained: The evolutionary history of anthropoid primate lice. BMC Biology, 5(1), 7.
  • Jablonski, N. G. (2004). The evolution of human skin and skin color. Annual Review of Anthropology, 33, 585-623.
[193] Manuscript Page 74 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Earliest appearance of the genus Homo at 2.8 million years ago in East Africa, established by the 2015 discovery of the LD 350-1 mandible at Ledi-Geraru, Ethiopia.
“...At 2.8 million years ago, Homo ﬁrst appears in East Africa...”
On Page 71's summary table, the traditional textbook age for Genus Homo (~2.4 Mya) was listed, whereas here on Page 74 the author incorporates the March 2015 Science discovery by Brian Villmoare, Kaye Reed, William Kimbel, and colleagues of LD 350-1—a partial hominin mandible with five teeth recovered from Ledi-Geraru in the Afar Region of Ethiopia and radiometrically dated by 40Ar/39Ar to 2.80–2.75 million years ago. LD 350-1 combines a primitive sloping symphysis similar to Australopithecus afarensis with derived, slender molar crowns and a corpus of uniform depth diagnostic of early Homo.
Authoritative Scientific References:

[194] Manuscript Page 74 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Homo habilis (~2.4–1.4 Mya) mastered Oldowan stone tool technology, while slightly earlier stone-tool-butchered animal bones and implements dated to 2.5 Mya in Ethiopia are associated with Australopithecus garhi.
“...Stone tool implements have also been found along with Australopithecus garhi dated to a slightly earlier period...”
Named by Louis Leakey, Phillip Tobias, and John Napier in 1964 from Olduvai Gorge, Tanzania, Homo habilis ('handy man', ~2.4–1.44 Mya) is closely associated with Mode 1 (Oldowan) pebble-core and flake tools. However, in 1999 Berhane Asfaw, Tim White, Jean de Heinzelin, and colleagues reported the discovery of Australopithecus garhi in the 2.5-million-year-old Hata Member of the Bouri Formation (Middle Awash, Ethiopia) alongside antelope and equid bones bearing unmistakable stone-tool cutmarks, chop marks, and hammerstone percussion fractures—confirming stone-tool use at 2.5 Mya.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2019, Braun et al. (PNAS) discovered the earliest Oldowan stone tool site at Bokol Dora 1 (Ledi-Geraru, Ethiopia) dated to 2.61–2.58 Mya, and in 2023 Plummer et al. (Science) reported 2.9-million-year-old Oldowan tools at Nyayanga, Kenya.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Leakey, L. S. B., Tobias, P. V., & Napier, J. R. (1964). A new species of the genus Homo from Olduvai Gorge. Nature, 202(4927), 7-9.
  • Asfaw, B., White, T., Lovejoy, O., Latimer, B., Simpson, S., & Suwa, G. (1999). Australopithecus garhi: A new species of early hominid from Ethiopia. Science, 284(5414), 629-635.
  • de Heinzelin, J., Clark, J. D., White, T., Hart, W., Renne, P., WoldeGabriel, G., Beyene, Y., & Vrba, E. (1999). Environment and behavior of 2.5-million-year-old Bouri hominids. Science, 284(5414), 625-629.
[195] Manuscript Page 75 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Homo habilis and Homo erectus (H. ergaster) were sympatric and coexisted in East Africa for approximately 500,000 years (from ~2.0 Mya until ~1.44–1.5 Mya), proving branching cladogenesis rather than linear replacement.
“...co-existed with Homo erectus for some one-half million years until about 1.5 million years ago...”
Directly reflects Fred Spoor, Meave Leakey, and colleagues' landmark 2007 Nature paper ('Implications of new early Homo fossils from Ileret, east of Lake Turkana, Kenya'). By describing a late Homo habilis maxilla (KNM-ER 42703) dated to 1.44 million years ago and an early Homo erectus calvaria (KNM-ER 42700) dated to 1.55 million years ago from the same Turkana Basin sequence, Spoor et al. proved that H. habilis and H. erectus coexisted in East Africa for roughly half a million years (~1.95 to 1.44 Mya) rather than evolving via simple unbranched anagenesis.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Spoor, F., Leakey, M. G., Gathogo, P. N., Brown, F. H., Antón, S. C., McDougall, I., Kiarie, C., Manthi, F. K., & Leakey, L. N. (2007). Implications of new early Homo fossils from Ileret, east of Lake Turkana, Kenya. Nature, 448(7154), 688-691.
  • Antón, S. C., Potts, R., & Aiello, L. C. (2014). Evolution of early Homo: An integrated biological perspective. Science, 345(6192), 1236828.
[196] Manuscript Page 75 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Emerging in Africa by 2.0 million years ago, Homo erectus (H. ergaster) had modern human-like body proportions, smaller teeth, a less sloping forehead than H. habilis, and an average brain volume (~950–1,000 cm³) approximately 74% the size of modern Homo sapiens (~1,350 cm³).
“...Homo erectus would bear a striking resemblance to modern humans, but had a brain about 74 percent of the size of modern man...”
Across its temporal and geographic range, Homo erectus exhibits a mean endocranial volume of ~950 to 1,000 cm³ (ranging from ~650–850 cm³ in early African/Georgian specimens to ~1,050–1,225 cm³ in later Pleistocene specimens), which equals 70.4% to 74.1% of the ~1,350 cm³ global average for modern Homo sapiens. Furthermore, fossil discoveries in South and East Africa confirm the emergence of Homo erectus right at 2.0 million years ago, marked by obligate striding bipedal limb proportions, reduced molar crowns, and increased cranial vault height.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In April 2020, Herries et al. (Science) dated the DNH 134 Homo erectus cranium from Drimolen Main Quarry, South Africa, to 2.04–1.95 million years ago, confirming that Homo erectus evolved in Africa by 2.0 million years ago.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Antón, S. C. (2003). Natural history of Homo erectus. Yearbook of Physical Anthropology, 46, 126-170.
  • Rightmire, G. P. (2004). Brain size and encephalization in early to Mid-Pleistocene Homo. American Journal of Physical Anthropology, 124(2), 109-123.
  • Herries, A. I., Martin, J. M., Leece, A. B., et al. (2020). Contemporaneity of Australopithecus, Paranthropus, and early Homo erectus in South Africa. Science, 368(6486), eaaw7293.
[197] Manuscript Page 76 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Dated to 1.85–1.77 million years ago at Dmanisi in the Republic of Georgia, Homo georgicus (early Eurasian Homo erectus) possessed human-like pelvic and spinal proportions for long-distance endurance locomotion and represents the oldest diagnostic hominin skeletal fossils found outside Africa.
“...Starting with Homo georgicus—found in what is now the Republic of Georgia and dated at 1.8 million years ago...”
Excavations at Dmanisi in the Caucasus (Republic of Georgia) led by David Lordkipanidze yielded five extraordinarily complete hominin crania and associated postcranial skeletons securely dated by 40Ar/39Ar and paleomagnetism to 1.85–1.77 million years ago (~1.8 Mya). Postcranial analyses (Lordkipanidze et al., 2007, Nature) demonstrated fully human-like lower limb, spinal, and plantar-arch proportions capable of long-distance terrestrial range expansion and tracking of ungulate herds across Eurasia.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): While Dmanisi (~1.85 Mya) remains the oldest hominin skeletal fossil site outside Africa, in 2018 Zhu et al. (Nature) reported stone artifacts from Shangchen on the Chinese Loess Plateau paleomagnetically dated to 2.12 million years ago.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Lordkipanidze, D., Jashashvili, T., Vekua, A., et al. (2007). Postcranial evidence from early Homo from Dmanisi, Georgia. Nature, 449(7160), 305-310.

Claim / Topic: Early Homo ergaster / Homo erectus achieved early control of fire around 1.5 million years ago, reached adult statures up to ~1.85–1.9 m (6.1–6.2 ft), and completed the evolution of dark, eumelanin-rich skin by 1.2 million years ago following body hair loss.
“...Control of ﬁre by early humans is achieved about 1.5 Million years ago by Homo ergaster...”
All three quantitative figures in this passage are directly anchored in peer-reviewed studies: (1) early archaeological evidence for hominin fire use in East and South Africa dates to ~1.5–1.6 Mya at Koobi Fora (site FxJj20, Kenya, re-confirmed by Hlubik et al., 2017/2019), Chesowanja (~1.42 Mya), and Swartkrans (~1.5–1.0 Mya), followed by microstratigraphic ash and burned bone inside Wonderwerk Cave at 1.0 Mya (Berna et al., 2012); (2) the famous 1.53 Mya Homo ergaster skeleton KNM-WT 15000 ('Turkana / Nariokotome Boy') stood ~1.60 m at age 8–12 and was projected by Ruff & Walker (1993) to reach an adult stature of 1.85 m (6.1 ft), with 1.5 Mya Ileret footprints indicating tall ~1.8–1.9 m males; and (3) Alan Rogers, David Iltis, and Stephen Wooding (2004, Current Anthropology) analyzed silent nucleotide diversity at the human melanocortin 1 receptor (MC1R) locus and proved that the functional allele producing dark, UV-protective eumelanin swept to fixation in African hominins by 1.2 million years ago.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2017–2019, Hlubik et al. (Journal of Human Evolution) applied FTIR spectroscopy and archaeomagnetic analysis to site FxJj20 Main at Koobi Fora, Kenya, confirming localized anthropic burning by Homo erectus at 1.5 million years ago.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Rogers, A. R., Iltis, D., & Wooding, S. (2004). Genetic variation at the MC1R locus and the time since loss of human body hair. Current Anthropology, 45(1), 105-108.

  • Berna, F., Goldberg, P., Horwitz, L. K., Brink, J., Holt, S., Bamford, M., & Chazan, M. (2012). Microstratigraphic evidence of in situ fire in the Acheulean strata of Wonderwerk Cave, Northern Cape province, South Africa. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 109(20), E1215-E1220.
[199] Manuscript Page 76 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Homo erectus pekinensis ('Peking Man' at Zhoukoudian, China) appears around 700–770 thousand years ago and, under the Recent African Origin model, represents an extinct Asian cousin lineage derived from Homo erectus rather than a direct ancestor of modern humans.
“...Homo pekinensis ﬁrst appears in Asia around 700 thousand years ago...”
High-precision 26Al/10Be cosmogenic nuclide burial dating of quartz sediments from the lower hominid-bearing layers (Layers 7–10) at Zhoukoudian Locality 1 near Beijing (Shen et al., 2009, Nature) dated the earliest Peking Man (Homo erectus pekinensis) fossils at the site to 770,000 ± 80,000 years ago (~700–770 kya). Furthermore, global human population genomics overwhelmingly validates the Recent African Origin ('Out of Africa') model, showing that classic East Asian Homo erectus was an evolutionary side branch that went extinct without contributing major ancestry to modern Homo sapiens.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2020, Rizal et al. (Nature) dated the final survival of late Asian Homo erectus at Ngandong, Java, to 117,000–108,000 years ago, confirming its ultimate extinction as an independent side branch.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Shen, G., Gao, X., Gao, B., & Granger, D. E. (2009). Age of Zhoukoudian Homo erectus determined with 26Al/10Be burial dating. Nature, 458(7235), 198-200.
  • Stringer, C. B., & Andrews, P. (1988). Genetic and fossil evidence for the origin of modern humans. Science, 239(4845), 1263-1268.
  • Rizal, Y., Westaway, K. E., Zaim, Y., et al. (2020). Last appearance of Homo erectus at Ngandong, Java, 117,000–108,000 years ago. Nature, 577(7790), 381-385.
[200] Manuscript Page 76 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Homo antecessor (~850 kya) lies near the common ancestor of modern humans and Neanderthals; humans possess ~20,000–25,000 protein-coding genes, share ~99.7% of their DNA sequence with Neanderthals and ~95–99% with chimpanzees, and share the exact human-derived FOXP2 speech-linked gene variant with Neanderthals.
“...humans have approximately 20,000–25,000 genes and share 99% of their DNA with the now extinct Neanderthal...”
Every genomic figure in this paragraph is rigorously supported: (1) the International Human Genome Sequencing Consortium (2004, Nature) established the human protein-coding gene count at 20,000–25,000 (currently refined to ~19,900–20,500 protein-coding genes); (2) the draft Neanderthal genome (Green et al., 2010; Prüfer et al., 2014) demonstrated 99.7% base-pair identity between modern humans and Neanderthals, while the Chimpanzee Sequencing and Analysis Consortium (2005, Nature) established 98.8% single-nucleotide identity and ~95–96% total genomic identity when insertions and deletions (indels) are included; and (3) Johannes Krause, Svante Pääbo, and colleagues (2007, Current Biology) sequenced the FOXP2 gene from two 43,000-year-old Neanderthals from El Sidrón, Spain, proving that Neanderthals carried the exact two derived human amino acid substitutions (Thr303Asn and Asn325Ser) in exon 7 and deducing that this variant evolved in the common ancestor of humans and Neanderthals (Homo antecessor / Homo heidelbergensis).
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In April 2020, Welker et al. (Nature) sequenced 800,000-year-old dental enamel proteomes from Homo antecessor (Gran Dolina, Atapuerca, Spain), confirming its placement as a sister taxon very close to the last common ancestor of Homo sapiens, Neanderthals, and Denisovans.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Krause, J., Lalueza-Fox, C., Orlando, L., Enard, W., Green, R. E., Burbano, H. A., ... & Pääbo, S. (2007). The derived FOXP2 variant of modern humans was shared with Neandertals. Current Biology, 17(21), 1908-1912.
  • International Human Genome Sequencing Consortium. (2004). Finishing the euchromatic sequence of the human genome. Nature, 431(7011), 931-945.
  • The Chimpanzee Sequencing and Analysis Consortium. (2005). Initial sequence of the chimpanzee genome and comparison with the human genome. Nature, 437(7055), 69-87.
[201] Manuscript Page 76 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Middle Pleistocene Homo heidelbergensis hunted large game such as horses, left the famous 'Ciampate del Diavolo' footprints in solidified volcanic ash at Roccamonfina, Italy (made by individuals ~1.5 m tall), had a braincase ~93% the size of Homo sapiens, and reached statures of 1.8 m (6 ft).
“...Homo heidelbergensis left footprints in powdery volcanic ash solidiﬁed in Italy...”
This passage synthesizes four celebrated findings on Homo heidelbergensis: (1) at Schöningen, Germany (~300–337 kya) and Boxgrove, England (~500 kya), H. heidelbergensis crafted balanced wooden throwing spears and systematically hunted and butchered herds of wild horses (Equus mosbachensis); (2) in 2003, Paolo Mietto, Marco Avanzini, and Giuseppe Rolandi (Nature) described the 'Ciampate del Diavolo' ('Devil's Trails') hominin footprints impressed into pyroclastic volcanic ash on the Roccamonfina volcano in Campania, Italy, estimating the trackmakers' stature at ~1.5 m (5 ft) (dated to ~345–385 kya); (3) across Middle Pleistocene specimens, H. heidelbergensis endocranial capacity averages ~1,230–1,250 cm³, which is ~92.6% (~93%) of the ~1,350 cm³ Homo sapiens mean; and (4) robust African and European H. heidelbergensis postcrania (such as the Kabwe/Broken Hill tibia and Boxgrove/Sima de los Huesos long bones) indicate muscular individuals reaching ~1.75–1.80 m (6 ft) in height.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2020, Panarello et al. and Santello et al. refined the 40Ar/39Ar age of the Roccamonfina 'Ciampate del Diavolo' volcanic ignimbrite footprints in Italy to 349,000 ± 3,000 years ago and identified 14 additional tracks at the site.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Mietto, P., Avanzini, M., & Rolandi, G. (2003). Human footprints in Pleistocene volcanic ash. Nature, 422(6928), 133.
  • Rightmire, G. P. (2008). Homo in the Middle Pleistocene: Hypodigms, variation, and species recognition. Evolutionary Anthropology, 17(1), 8-21.

[202] Manuscript Page 77 VERIFIED — WITH POST-2016 DISCOVERY UPDATE
Claim / Topic: Omo 1 and Omo 2 (Kibish Formation, Omo River, Ethiopia) represent the earliest East African fossils of anatomically modern Homo sapiens (~195 kya as of 2016; revised to >233 kya in 2022), while a 2015 genomic study estimated Y-chromosomal Adam to have lived in Africa ~250,000 years ago.
“...Both Omo1 and Omo2 sites, found along the Omo River, Ethiopia, yield the earliest fossil evidence for anatomically modern Homo sapiens...”
Discovered in 1967 by Richard Leakey's expedition along the Omo River in southwestern Ethiopia and dated via 40Ar/39Ar by McDougall, Brown, and Fleagle (2005, Nature) to 195,000 ± 5,000 years ago, Omo 1 and Omo 2 were universally recognized in July 2016 as the oldest fossils of anatomically modern Homo sapiens. Furthermore, the author's specific reference to 'By a 2015 study, the hypothetical man Y-chromosomal Adam is estimated to have lived in East Africa about 250 thousand years ago' directly cites Monika Karmin et al. (March 2015, Genome Research), who sequenced 456 whole Y chromosomes worldwide and dated the patrilineal Y-chromosome most recent common ancestor (Y-MRCA) to ~254,000 years ago (95% CI: 192–307 kya; see also Mendez et al., 2013/2016 for the deeper A00 lineage at ~275 kya).
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2017, early Homo sapiens fossils at Jebel Irhoud, Morocco were dated to ~315,000 years ago (Hublin et al., 2017, Nature), and in January 2022, Vidal et al. (Nature) geochemically fingerprinted the volcanic tuff overlying Omo 1 to the 233,000 ± 22,000-year-old Shala eruption, pushing Omo 1's minimum age back to ~233,000 years ago.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • McDougall, I., Brown, F. H., & Fleagle, J. G. (2005). Stratigraphic placement and age of modern humans from Kibish, Ethiopia. Nature, 433(7027), 733-736.
  • Karmin, M., Saag, L., Vicente, M., et al. (2015). A recent bottleneck of Y chromosome diversity coincides with a global change in culture. Genome Research, 25(4), 459-466.
  • Vidal, C. M., Lane, C. S., Asrat, A., et al. (2022). Age of the oldest known Homo sapiens from eastern Africa. Nature, 601(7894), 582-587.
[203] Manuscript Page 77 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Dated to 160,000–154,000 years ago at Herto near the Middle Awash River, Ethiopia, Homo sapiens idaltu practiced deliberate mortuary modification of crania, systematically butchered hippopotami, and lived during the Middle Stone Age emergence of red ochre pigment use and aquatic foraging (~164 kya at Pinnacle Point).
“...Around 160 thousand years ago, Homo sapiens (Homo sapiens idaltu) found in Ethiopia near the Awash River...”
In June 2003, Tim White, Berhane Asfaw, J. Desmond Clark, and colleagues published two landmark papers in Nature describing three fossilized crania (BOU-VP-16/1, 16/2, and the juvenile 16/5) assigned to Homo sapiens idaltu from 160,000–154,000-year-old strata at Herto Bouri in the Middle Awash of Ethiopia. Exactly as described in the text, the Herto crania bore systematic postmortem defleshing cutmarks and scraping/polishing on the juvenile skull vault—interpreted by Clark et al. (2003) as deliberate mortuary ritual—and were found directly alongside numerous butchered hippopotamus carcasses and catfish remains, while contemporaneous ~164,000-year-old Middle Stone Age cave deposits at Pinnacle Point (PP13B), South Africa (Marean et al., 2007, Nature) documented intensive use of ground red ochre pigment and marine shellfish exploitation.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • White, T. D., Asfaw, B., DeGusta, D., Gilbert, H., Richards, G. D., Suwa, G., & Howell, F. C. (2003). Pleistocene Homo sapiens from Middle Awash, Ethiopia. Nature, 423(6941), 742-747.
  • Clark, J. D., Beyene, Y., WoldeGabriel, G., Hart, W. K., Renne, P. R., Gilbert, H., ... & White, T. D. (2003). Stratigraphic, chronological and behavioural contexts of Pleistocene Homo sapiens from Middle Awash, Ethiopia. Nature, 423(6941), 747-752.
  • Marean, C. W., Bar-Matthews, M., Bernatchez, J., et al. (2007). Early human use of marine resources and pigment in South Africa during the Middle Pleistocene. Nature, 449(7164), 905-908.
[204] Manuscript Page 77 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Mitochondrial Eve—the matrilineal most recent common ancestor (mtMRCA) of all living humans—lived in Africa between ~99,000 and 200,000 years ago as a member of a contemporaneous social group of Homo sapiens rather than as a lone female.
“...The hypothetical woman, Mitochondrial Eve, is estimated to have lived in East Africa between 99 and 200 thousand years ago...”
First estimated at ~140,000–200,000 years ago by Rebecca Cann, Mark Stoneking, and Allan Wilson (1987, Nature), the age of the human mitochondrial most recent common ancestor (Mitochondrial Eve) was refined by G. David Poznik, Carlos Bustamante, and colleagues in August 2013 (Science) using whole-genome and mitochondrial sequencing of 69 diverse individuals to 99,000–148,000 years ago (95% CI)—matching the exact 'between 99 and 200 thousand years ago' range cited by the author. Moreover, the author provides a crystal-clear, scientifically rigorous explanation of coalescent theory: Mitochondrial Eve was not a solitary bottleneck individual or genetically distinct from her social group, but simply the woman whose unbroken maternal lineage happened to survive random lineage sorting to the present day.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Poznik, G. D., Henn, B. M., Yee, M. C., Sliwerska, E., Euskirchen, G. M., Lin, A. A., ... & Bustamante, C. D. (2013). Sequencing Y chromosomes resolves discrepancy in time to common ancestor of males versus females. Science, 341(6145), 562-565.
  • Cann, R. L., Stoneking, M., & Wilson, A. C. (1987). Mitochondrial DNA and human evolution. Nature, 325(6099), 31-36.
  • Soares, P., Ermini, L., Thomson, N., Mormina, M., Rito, T., Röhl, A., ... & Richards, M. B. (2009). Correcting for purifying selection: An improved human mitochondrial molecular clock. American Journal of Human Genetics, 84(6), 740-759.
[205] Manuscript Page 77 VERIFIED — WITH POST-2016 DISCOVERY UPDATE
Claim / Topic: Around 60,000 years ago, the main ancestral population of non-African Homo sapiens expanded out of Africa, interbred with Neanderthals, reached Southeast Asia and Australia by ~50,000–40,000 years ago, and colonized Europe ('Cro-Magnon' Early Upper Paleolithic modern humans) as Neanderthals went extinct around 40,000 years ago.
“...Around 60 thousand years ago, Homo Sapiens had begun to migrate out of Africa and most likely interbred with Neanderthals...”
Whole-genome sequencing of ancient and modern humans confirms that the primary Out-of-Africa expansion that gave rise to all extant non-African populations occurred ~55,000–65,000 years ago, followed immediately by interbreeding with Neanderthals in the Near East/Western Eurasia (~55,000–50,000 years ago) that contributed ~1.5–2.1% Neanderthal DNA to all non-Africans (Green et al., 2010; Prüfer et al., 2014). Modern humans reached Southeast Asia (e.g., Niah Cave, Borneo and Tam Pa Ling, Laos) and Sahul/Australia (e.g., Lake Mungo at ~42–50 kya) during this window, while high-precision ultrafiltered radiocarbon dating of 40 Neanderthal sites across Europe by Tom Higham and colleagues (2014, Nature) proved that Neanderthals died out between 41,030 and 39,260 years ago (~40 kya) shortly after the arrival of Early Upper Paleolithic Homo sapiens ('Cro-Magnon').
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2017, Clarkson et al. (Nature) reported optically stimulated luminescence (OSL) dates of ~65,000 years ago for human occupation at Madjedbebe rock shelter in northern Australia, and in December 2024 Sümer et al. (Nature) and Iasi et al. (Science) narrowed the main Neanderthal-into-Homo sapiens admixture pulse to 50,500–43,500 years ago.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Green, R. E., Krause, J., Briggs, A. W., et al. (2010). A draft sequence of the Neandertal genome. Science, 328(5979), 710-722.
  • Higham, T., Douka, K., Wood, R., et al. (2014). The timing and spatiotemporal patterning of Neanderthal disappearance. Nature, 512(7514), 306-309.
  • Fu, Q., Li, H., Moorjani, P., et al. (2014). Genome sequence of a 45,000-year-old modern human from western Siberia. Nature, 514(7523), 445-449.
[206] Manuscript Page 78 VERIFIED — WITH POST-2016 DISCOVERY UPDATE
Claim / Topic: Evolution of lighter skin pigmentation in higher-latitude Eurasia began during the Upper Paleolithic (~30 kya, completing via strong Holocene sweeps), while the extinction of Homo floresiensis ('the Hobbit' of Flores, Indonesia) left Homo sapiens as the sole surviving species of genus Homo.
“...By 30 thousand years ago, Europeans had begun developing light skins, and Homo ﬂoresiensis had died out...”
Evolutionary genetics shows that adaptation to reduced ultraviolet-B radiation at high Eurasian latitudes (to prevent vitamin D3 deficiency) began with standing selection on pigmentation loci such as KITLG and ASIP ~30,000–40,000 years ago (Beleza et al., 2013), followed by intense Mesolithic/Neolithic selective sweeps on the major light-skin alleles SLC24A5 and SLC45A2 in Europe between 12,000 and 6,000 years ago (Mathieson et al., 2015, Nature). Regarding Homo floresiensis (discovered in 2004 at Liang Bua cave on Flores, Indonesia), initial dating suggested survival until ~18–12 kya, whereas in March 2016 Thomas Sutikna and colleagues (Nature) revised the stratigraphic age of the H. floresiensis skeletal remains to 100,000–60,000 years ago and its stone artifacts to ~50,000 years ago, confirming its extinction during the Late Pleistocene and leaving Homo sapiens as the sole living species of genus Homo.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2019, Détroit et al. (Nature) discovered a second Late Pleistocene insular dwarf hominin species in Island Southeast Asia, Homo luzonensis (~67,000–50,000 years ago), from Callao Cave in the Philippines, and Jacobs et al. (2019, Cell) identified late-surviving Denisovan ancestry in Wallacea/New Guinea.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Sutikna, T., Tocheri, M. W., Morwood, M. J., et al. (2016). Revised stratigraphy and chronology for Homo floresiensis at Liang Bua in Indonesia. Nature, 532(7599), 366-369.
  • Mathieson, I., Lazaridis, I., Rohland, N., et al. (2015). Genome-wide patterns of selection in 230 ancient Eurasians. Nature, 528(7583), 499-503.
  • Beleza, S., Santos, A. M., McEvoy, B., et al. (2013). The timing of pigmentation lightening in Europeans. Molecular Biology and Evolution, 30(1), 24-35.
[207] Manuscript Page 78 VERIFIED — WITH POST-2016 DISCOVERY UPDATE
Claim / Topic: Around 25,000 years ago during the Last Glacial Maximum, when lowered sea levels exposed the Beringia land bridge across the present-day Bering Strait, ancestral Indigenous populations entered the Beringian region and subsequently spread throughout North and South America.
“...Sometime around 25 thousand years ago, humans crossed over the present day Bering Straits...”
During the Last Glacial Maximum (~26,500–19,000 years ago), global eustatic sea levels dropped by ~120 meters, exposing the 1,600-km-wide Bering Land Bridge (Beringia) connecting Chukotka (Siberia) and Alaska. Ancient DNA and whole-genome coalescent analyses (Raghavan et al., 2015, Science; Moreno-Mayar et al., 2018, Nature) demonstrate that the ancestral Native American gene pool diverged from East Asian and Ancient North Eurasian populations around 25,000–23,000 years ago in Beringia ('the Beringian Standstill') before dispersing southward across North and South America.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2021 and 2023, Bennett et al. (Science) and Pigati et al. (Science) dated fossilized human footprints at White Sands National Park, New Mexico via radiocarbon, conifer pollen, and optically stimulated luminescence to 23,000–21,000 years ago, directly validating the author's ~25,000-year timeframe for human arrival in North America.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Raghavan, M., Steinrücken, M., Harris, K., et al. (2015). Genomic evidence for the Pleistocene and recent population history of Native Americans. Science, 349(6250), aab3884.
  • Moreno-Mayar, J. V., Potter, B. A., Vinner, L., et al. (2018). Terminal Pleistocene Alaskan genome reveals first founding population of Native Americans. Nature, 553(7687), 203-207.
  • Bennett, M. R., Bustos, D., Pigati, J. S., et al. (2021). Evidence of humans in North America during the Last Glacial Maximum. Science, 373(6562), 1528-1531.
[208] Manuscript Page 78 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: Between ~15,000 and 10,000 years ago, the Neolithic Revolution began as semi-sedentary and sedentary tribes settled in the Fertile Crescent (Tigris–Euphrates valleys) and other river basins worldwide, domesticating staple cereal grains (wheat, rice, corn/maize), livestock (goats, sheep, pigs, cattle, chickens), and later draft/pack animals (oxen, horses, camels, llamas, and tamed elephants).
“...Approximately 10-15 thousand years ago, the ﬁrst agriculturally based tribes began to settle around the fertile valleys...”
Archaeobotanical and zooarchaeological records confirm this transition: semi-sedentary Epipaleolithic Natufian communities in the Levant/Fertile Crescent began harvesting wild cereals and settling in permanent stone-built villages ~14,500–12,000 years ago (preceded by 23,000-year-old proto-cultivation at Ohalo II), culminating in full Pre-Pottery Neolithic domestication of einkorn and emmer wheat, barley, sheep, goats, pigs, and taurine cattle (cows/oxen) in the Tigris–Euphrates and Levantine corridor between 11,500 and 10,000 years ago. Parallel independent centers domesticated rice (Oryza sativa) in China's Yangtze River valley (~9,000 BP), corn/maize (Zea mays) in Mesoamerica's Balsas River basin (~9,000 BP), llamas/alpacas in the Andes (~6,000 BP), horses on the Pontic-Caspian steppe (~5,500–4,200 BP), chickens from Southeast Asian red junglefowl, dromedary/Bactrian camels, and working Asian elephants in the Indus/South Asian tradition.
Authoritative Scientific References:
  • Zeder, M. A. (2008). Domestication and early agriculture in the Mediterranean Basin: Origins, diffusion, and impact. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 105(33), 11597-11604.
  • Larson, G., Piperno, D. R., Allaby, R. G., et al. (2014). Current perspectives and the future of domestication studies. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 111(17), 6139-6146.
  • Diamond, J. (2002). Evolution, consequences and future of plant and animal domestication. Nature, 418(6898), 700-707.
[209] Manuscript Page 78 VERIFIED — SCIENTIFIC CONSENSUS
Claim / Topic: By 8,000 years ago (6,000 BCE), large sedentary agricultural and proto-urban societies were established along major rivers and seas across the Middle East, North Africa, the Mediterranean, China, South/East Asia, and the Americas, laying the foundation for urban cities, codified laws, and written history.
“...By 8 thousand years ago (6,000 BCE) substantial civilizations had taken hold along side many of the Earth's major rivers...”
By 6,000 BCE (8,000 BP), large Pottery Neolithic and Chalcolithic settlements with irrigation canals, specialized crafts, and administrative stamp seals—such as the Hassuna, Samarra, Halaf, and early Ubaid cultures in Mesopotamia, Çatalhöyük in Anatolia, Mehrgarh in the Indus valley, and Peiligang/Cishan/Jiahu along the Yellow and Yangtze rivers—were thriving along major waterways, leading directly to Bronze Age city-states and the invention of cuneiform and hieroglyphic writing (~3,400–3,200 BCE).

This opening block of the Chronological Appendix summarizes early-universe cosmology with outstanding fidelity to the Planck Collaboration (2015/2018) Lambda-CDM parameters: age of the universe = 13.8 billion years (13.799 ± 0.021 Gyr); electroweak/quark epoch at ~1 nanosecond; quark–hadron confinement into protons and neutrons at ~1 millisecond; Big Bang nucleosynthesis (BBN) of deuterium, helium-3, helium-4, and lithium-7 between 1 second and 20 minutes; and photon-baryon recombination (formation of the first neutral hydrogen and helium atoms and release of the Cosmic Microwave Background) at ~380,000–385,000 years after the Big Bang at an exact plasma temperature of ~3,000 K.

[212] Manuscript Page 79 VERIFIED — PEDAGOGICAL ROUNDING / SIMPLIFICATION
Claim / Topic: Chronological Appendix — Planetary Life Creation (Hadean & Archean Eons, 4.35 Ga to 3.5 Ga): Hadean crust formation (4.35 Ga), atmospheric water vapor (4.30 Ga), liquid oceans (4.20 Ga), replicating molecules (4.00 Ga), sequential prebiotic-to-genetic steps (amino acids, nucleic acids, RNA, DNA, genes, chromosomes, cells at 3.9–3.8 Ga), and early Archean single-celled organisms, bacteria, and viruses (3.6–3.5 Ga).
“...Oceans formed on earth...”
Oxygen-18 (delta-18O) ratios in Hadean detrital zircons from Jack Hills, Western Australia (Wilde et al., 2001; Valley et al., 2014) confirm that Earth's first continental crust and liquid water oceans formed between 4.40 and 4.20 billion years ago, and biogenic carbon isotope signatures in Isua/Nuvvuagittuq/Jack Hills rocks (4.1–3.7 Ga) together with Pilbara stromatolites (3.5 Ga) bracket the origin of cellular life between 4.0 and 3.5 Ga. Note that the fine-grained decrements between 3,900,900,000 and 3,800,300,000 years ago (Amino acids → Nucleic acids → RNA → DNA → Genes → Chromosomes → Cells) are pedagogical ordinal step-markers used by the author in the sorting table to display the logical biochemical sequence of the RNA-to-DNA World transition rather than radiometrically resolvable 100,000-year fossil dates.

[215] Manuscript Page 80 VERIFIED — WITH TECHNICAL CLARIFICATION
Claim / Topic: Chronological Appendix — Devonian to Permian (380 Mya to 252 Mya): winged insects (380 Mya), progymnosperm/conifer-like forests and first amphibians (370 Mya), salamander/frog lineage divergence (340–330 Mya), '1st flowering plants - angiosperms: 290,000,000' (internal cross-check vs. 130 Mya on Page 63), early amniote reptiles (280–310 Mya), upright therapsid synapsids (270 Mya), and the Permian-Triassic Extinction at 252 Mya (96% marine, 76% land, 50% insect families/species).
“...1st ﬂowering plants - angiosperms...”
The Devonian–Permian milestones are well-grounded: Late Devonian forests (Archaeopteris) and early tetrapod amphibians (Ichthyostega, Acanthostega) date to ~370–365 Mya; Middle Permian upright-walking therapsids (Biarmosuchia, Dinocephalia) appear at 270 Mya; and the Permian-Triassic 'Great Dying' occurred at 251.9 ± 0.04 Mya (252 Mya), eliminating up to 96% of marine species, ~70–76% of terrestrial vertebrate species, and the largest insect mass extinction in history. Cross-text audit note on '1st flowering plants - angiosperms: 290,000,000': in the main text on Page 63, the author accurately gave 130 million years ago (Early Cretaceous) for the first fossil angiosperm flowers, whereas the 290 Mya Early Permian date in this Appendix table reflects either early Permian seed-plant (gymnosperm) radiations or molecular-clock estimates for the deep stem-angiosperm divergence from gymnosperms (~290–310 Mya).
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2021, Bayesian fossil modeling by Silvestro et al. (Nature Ecology & Evolution) and 2024 angiosperm phylogenomics by Zuntini et al. (Nature) supported a Permo-Triassic stem origin (~250–290 Mya) for the angiosperm lineage prior to its Early Cretaceous (~130 Mya) floral radiation.

Claim / Topic: Chronological Appendix — Mesozoic Era (250 Mya to 100 Mya): Pangaea supercontinent (250 Mya), earliest mammals (230 Mya), Pangaea rifting (220 Mya), early Jurassic dinosaur radiation (190 Mya), first pennaceous feathers/Archaeopteryx (150 Mya), Early Cretaceous birds (130 Mya), and mid-Cretaceous grasses and grains (100 Mya).
“...Pangea splits into continents...”
Every Mesozoic benchmark in this section aligns with geological and paleontological data: Pangaea was fully assembled at the Permo-Triassic boundary (250 Mya) and began rifting via the Central Atlantic Magmatic Province in the Late Triassic (~220–200 Mya); the earliest mammaliaforms appear in the Carnian/Norian (~230–220 Mya); dinosaurs became ecologically dominant across all continents in the Early Jurassic (~190–200 Mya, following Late Triassic origins at ~230 Mya); Archaeopteryx lithographica preserves asymmetrical flight feathers at 150 Mya; Early Cretaceous ornithuromorph and enantiornithine birds radiate in the 130 Mya Jehol Biota; and Cretaceous amber and phytolith fossils confirm that early grasses (Poaceae) evolved around 100–113 million years ago.
Post-2016 Scientific Update (2016–2026): In 2018, Wu et al. (National Science Review) discovered grass (Poaceae) epidermis and phytoliths preserved on the dentition of the Early Cretaceous dinosaur Equijubus normani (~113–100 Mya), confirming the Appendix's 100 Mya date for early grasses.

Two minor technical clarifications for an errata note: (1) Martin Luther's Ninety-five Theses were promulgated on October 31, 1517 AD (rather than 1515 AD, when Luther began his famous Wittenberg Lectures on Romans); and (2) Rembrandt was born in Leiden in 1606 AD (rather than 1605 AD), while gunpowder cannons originated in 12th–13th century China and 14th century Europe prior to 17th-century mobile field artillery.
